> The cold here is a living thing. It presses from all sides. At night, when the wind dies, you can hear it. A silence so deep it has a sound of its own, like the ringing after a cannon blast.

On the localization — I took your instinct in its minimal form, **"the cold here,"** and I think it's a real improvement rather than a neutral variant, for a reason that serves the fragmenting itself: deixis converts the sentence from cosmic aphorism into regional report. "The cold is a living thing" is an essayist generalizing; "The cold here is a living thing" is a man in a specific place telling you a fact about it — which is exactly the direction the whole surgery pushes. It also rides canon that's already there: his own comparative baseline from November 14 ("worse than anything I had felt in Virginia or Mexico"), the trader's "the land doesn't like anyone" (the book's sanctioned, plainly settler-vernacular version of place-hostility — so Morrow agreeing from the other side of the country is earned convergence, not mythology), and the historically real 1856–57 starvation winter, where the localized claim happens to be empirically true.

The forms I rejected, for the record: **"this land's cold"** — the possessive gives the land ownership of its weather, which is the first step onto exactly the genius-loci ground you flagged; and **"in this country"** — period-flavored but three syllables against a line whose whole virtue is bluntness. "Here" personifies nothing; it just locates.

Ownership chain verified intact: the living-thing/presses-from-all-sides signature is still Morrow's alone, and 18:5 still rhymes against it on his last night. The registry row also got two stale pointers corrected while I was in there (08:63→08:71; the 12:117 instance actually died in the July-10 JE VI shrink and the row never noticed).

Still on the tier-2 shelf when you want them: the closer-uniformity program (the weightiest — wants a program-level ruling, not spot fixes), the DT-direction clash (degrade vs. earned-liturgy in Chapters 8–9), the Ch 9 closer two instruments disagree about, #36, and the 03:63 "By the time... By the time" anaphora break.

## ASSISTANT

**The issue.** Both register briefs, from opposite assignments, converged on the same structural finding: every one of the nineteen narrated units ends on a groomed curtain line, and — the deeper half — so do most of the dated entries *inside* Morrow's journal ("There is no point."; "There is no more use in me."; the weather-coming image). The prosecutor's formulation: "a field diary with the closural discipline of a composed novel is the deepest structural convergence — both narrators end paragraphs like the same author grooming cadence." It's also a documented Opus signature (the profile's "every section closing on a groomed cadence line"), so it carries model-fingerprint weight beyond the voice question. Both briefs prescribe the same asymmetry — Farrell exits flat and transactional, Morrow's entries allowed to *stop* rather than end — but each picked the other's narrator to cut first, which is why it needed a program ruling rather than spot fixes.

**My read, in four parts:**

*First, calibration:* no cold reader has ever complained about this. The regression readers praise specific closers (the Ch 6 ice paragraph is "the book's finest"; "I am going now." is untouchable). The pattern is visible only under the register lens and the tic-profile lens. So the harm isn't reader annoyance — it's that endings are the highest-salience sentences in a book, and if every boundary in both voices lands the same authorial chime, that's the "one pen" tell doing its damage exactly where memory forms.

*Second, the diegetic layer cuts asymmetrically, mostly in our favor already.* Farrell's account is retrospective and composed — a written book may close its chapters. Morrow's field diary is the harder side, but the worst offenders there — buttons on entries written in extremity — are precisely what we've already fixed: JE IV's stubs, the Jan 19 fragmenting, "And I will die." now ending the plan section, "Soon." What remains of Morrow's grooming sits mostly in rest-season entries, where the circumstance defense holds.

*Third, the key empirical fact: the slot regenerates its groom.* We replaced JE VI's closer last round and the replacement got flagged as groomed this round. Any line this model writes *into* the closing position comes out cadenced. So the only fix that works is **truncation, not rewriting** — delete the button and promote a sentence that was never written as an ending. Notice that today's batch quietly proved the method four times: the Ch 9 frame, "Then came Buena Vista," "I have become simpler," and the 16:69 cut were all closer-slot surgery by subtraction, and every one landed.

*Fourth, the program should break the pattern, not the instances.* De-grooming all nineteen units would be a new uniformity — and would cost earned lines. The pattern stops reading as authorial when a meaningful fraction of units end on un-groomed material. So: five targeted truncations, subtraction-only, split per the agreed asymmetry —

- **Ch 2:** cut "Everything before this had been prelude." — section ends on "In the morning, I would ride west toward the Mississippi and begin the real journey." Forward, logistical.
- **Ch 5:** cut "But the country had reminded me what it could do." — chapter ends on "A day more, maybe two." The perfect Farrell exit: an estimate.
- **Ch 9:** cut "One step at a time, one yard at a time." — ends on "But I kept riding."
- **JE II:** cut the chiasmus "I have nothing else to do. I have no one else to be." — ends on "however long it takes." (Peak-Opus chiasmus, and it's also on the wait-button list.)
- **JE V:** cut the second anaphora beat "Another winter, and this will be done." — ends on "Another winter, and she will be grown enough." One prophecy instead of a drumroll pair.

**Explicitly protected:** the ice paragraph, "I am going now.", "nothing left to follow", "the dead men following behind", JE VII's "I cannot wait any longer." (that's not decoration — it's the last line before the murder's white space), JE I's plain forward closer, and all of JE VI (floor declared). I'd leave "I did not look back." too — the defender flagged it, but it reads genre-native to me, and cutting it would promote a *worse* closer (the ice-and-distance weather image).

That's the program: five subtractions, both voices, no rewrites, keep-list intact. Say go and it's a twenty-minute batch with the usual shift discipline.

- the face shows sensual or erotic or obscene pleasure in the anticipated or actual goring

## ASSISTANT

This is the right pain point to unsettle — "His face was peaceful... He had gotten what he wanted" is the single sentence-cluster hostile v2 built both its McCarthy and Ballard indictments on, because it's the one place *the book's own narration* certifies that the sacrament worked. Here's how the options sit with me, the quick rejections first:

**Rejected:** *Discretion (3)* — Farrell itemizes the staved ribs two sentences later; a forensic narrator going coy about the face is inconsistent, and it would be a third prosecutable elision. *Positive expression (4)* — outright beatification; worse than peace. *Grotesque satisfaction (5)* — editorializes damnation with the same false authority that "peaceful" editorializes sanctity, plus melodrama. *Erotic/obscene (6)* — the commandment line works because the desire runs *under* the text; painting it on the corpse forecloses what the book lets readers find, and it breaks the restraint economy for a one-page shock. *No mention (8)* — the face is where every reader looks; conspicuous silence reads as flinch. *Scavengers (10)* — spends the buffalo's vigil (her presence is currently why the body is untouched, one of the few naturalistic props under the mythic license) and shifts attention from meaning to gore. *Worked over by men (11)* — this one I'd put a fence around: Lakota mutilation of a white corpse is the founding move of frontier-gothic anti-Indian propaganda, it detonates "no sign passed, of welcome or of warning," and no reading survives it. **(7)**, the pained/regretful face, is subtler but fails the same test as keeping it: a narrator who can read *doubt* in a frozen face is doing the identical certifying move, inverted — it settles the question the ending is built to keep open.

**The live option is your (2), and the book has already built the mechanism for it.** The frozen dead of this book have a face-word, and it isn't "peaceful" — it's **"calm."** The young man Farrell dug from the draw: "His face was calm. Whatever had killed him, he had not fought it at the end." And as of this morning, Chapter Eight: "his face had been calm." Twice established: *calm is what freezing does to a face.* If Morrow's face reads the same way, the certification collapses into an observation — and the rereader gets the devastating counter free: every frozen dead man looks like this; the peace is the cold's, not the sacrament's. No new machinery, no narrator self-flagging, no glass-tapping callback — the debunking is already loaded in Chapter Five and fires silently.

So my recommendation is subtraction plus one word:

> I knelt beside him and brushed the snow from his face.
>
> His face was calm. His eyes were open, staring up at the gray sky.
>
> His chest was crushed, the ribs staved in, the shape of his body wrong in ways that spoke of massive force. ...

The cuts: "the absence of pain or fear or anything else" (an impossible total-forensic claim — nobody can see the absence of *anything* in a face); **"He had gotten what he wanted."** — the verdict, the consolation machine's central gear, and the sentence every hostile quotes; and "The rest of him was not so peaceful," the groomed pivot that depends on the departed word. What stays: the kneel and the brushed snow, the open eyes on the gray sky (cold, unreadable, the right image), and the full wound inventory now juxtaposed against the calm face with no narration between them.

Two side benefits: the word "peace" now enters Farrell's Chapter Eleven exactly once — at 20:33, the sanctioned borrow — instead of being pre-spent on the corpse, which cleans the ownership chain; and the physiology stops asking for mythic license (a serene face after a goring was always the buffalo cooperating with her own symbolism one last time).

It does *push on* #29 without opening it: the ending's structure, the vision, the gold light, both peace sentences all stand — we're removing only the narrator's ratification of Morrow's account, which is the direction you set ("anti-consolation as we work on other stuff"). Say the word.

Landed and committed (92ba473). Two answers you asked for:

**On (9), the unreadable-by-injury face** — your guess is exactly right: it's coherent, not rejectable. It's honest to the physics, it kills the certification, and it even has a thematic teeth the others lack: the mechanism Morrow chose erases the face, the site of meaning — the buffalo declining to cooperate with her own symbolism one final time, which would answer the Melville indictment directly. I passed on it because it's *dominated*, not wrong: the "calm" move buys the same openness at near-zero cost, while (9) spends the brushed-snow beat, the open-eyes-on-gray-sky image, and it puts Catherine's ghost standing composed over gore the vision register isn't built to hold. If a future round ever shows "calm" isn't landing — if readers keep taking the face as benediction anyway — (9) is the escalation path, and it's now implicitly on file.

**On whether Morrow would shave or go naked** — no and no, and what's satisfying is that both the system and the canon already answer, independently:

*The system says no.* Purification is a claim — that the penitent can be made presentable — and Morrow's entire theology denies him that ("There is no absolution for what I am"). A man who believes he cannot be made clean does not wash for the altar. And any invented rite would breach his one scrupulously maintained boundary: at the offering stake, his mode is reverent non-participation — "I did not touch it. I did not camp near it." Shaving his head before the sacred animal would be playing at ceremony he knows isn't his; the book has kept him outside that line for three years and he knows it. Nakedness fails on his own stated terms: the cold killing him first is precisely the failure he refused in the rain ("that is not the death I am waiting for"), and the plan requires a body warm enough to run and shout — he needs *her* agency, not exposure's.

*The canon agrees.* The corpse wears coat and shirt (that's what the goring tears through); the beard is on him to the last — it's the rumor chain's identifying mark, and the morrow-hollow plate shows it on the final night; and his knife has been dull since 1857 anyway.

But here's the part I hadn't seen until your question made me look: **he does have a last rite, and it's already in the text — divestment.** The camp Farrell finds is a man's complete estate, abandoned: the journal staged on the blankets "as if left to be found," the buffalo robe rolled against the wall (he goes to her having given back her kind's hide), the knife, the cup, the burned-out light. He walked down owning nothing — JE VI's "everything I own rolls into one blanket" completed past zero. And the one thing he *did* groom for the encounter is the account itself, curated exactly as §3b documents. He prepares like a man closing a ledger, not a priest robing: books in order, estate abandoned, the body walked down as-is because the body is only the instrument to be destroyed. That's now in the character file as §3c, so the question stays answered.

## The inventory question: what's left, textually

There is a tier-3+, and it's short. After the tier-2 items, the complete remaining textual map is:

- **Taste items, yours:** #36 echo-pair (tally 3–2 against the unmarked peace; the make-quotation-explicit variant is on file); the Ch 9 closer ("Only the living made noise..." — defender wants it as the ending, hostile named it most self-satisfied); the 20:51 gray-to-gold light (flagged once, lean keep).
- **Structural/length shelf (real but heavy):** Ch 4 runs long by one episode; Ch 10's re-summary of the journal "costs a page"; JE V mild drag. Each is a half-session of surgery, not a line fix.
- **Micro/opportunistic:** two remaining wait-buttons (01:199, 15:151); the "I did not have a name for it then" modern-idiom edge in JE III; the small 03:63 "By the time... By the time" anaphora (still open from tier-2, five-minute fix whenever).
- **Agenda without a target:** Farrell's dangerousness asserted, never shown at its brink — the regression itself ruled it "a want, not a need," and it waits for a natural hook rather than an insertion.
- **Charges we carry as certified design costs:** the Melville symbol-never-fights-back, Lakota-as-furniture (with v2's "must cost something on the page" caveat noted), the Maxwell elision (compiler counter on file), buffalo-biology lampshade, Broken Oar smirk.
- **Held:** #29, accruing ammunition by design.

That's everything. No hidden drawer — the stack is the whole of it, and the book is genuinely close to textual quiescence outside the items you're deliberately sitting on.

## The DT-direction ruling

I ruled for the defender, and I think it's not close, on three grounds:

**Tense architecture.** The prosecutor's program — visions should degrade Farrell's prose — mistakes the experiencing instance for the narrating one. Morrow's journal is written *in* the fire; degradation there is honest and we've built it (the stubs, the fragmenting). Farrell's chapters are retrospective throughout — the composure is the survivor's, not the sufferer's. Breaking that would also break the account-as-composed-document ambiguity that underwrites the Q9 survival reading, the compiler question, and, for that matter, your fanon.

**Period.** An 1858 man does not render delirium tremens clinically — he renders visitations. "The horrors" in period diction is on the preserve list, and regression-3 already warned the psychology "maps to a casebook" too cleanly; the *clinical-and-ugly* register the prosecutor wants is the modern one. Ghosts with dignity are the period truth of the experience.

**The certified stroke.** Hostile v2's own concession crowns exactly this material — "the ghosts arrive on a chemical schedule and are never explained away... the book's own invention." You don't trade your most-certified original stroke for a register theory. And the prosecutor's one constructive demand — a *marked* borrowing at Ch 11 — is already satisfied by the existing architecture: quotation planted at 19:41, absorbed unmarked at 20:57.

**The residue that was true, applied:** the Ch 8 dream lost "—dark and deep" (that was Morrow's liquid-dark eye formula sitting on pre-reading Farrell; "eyes that were not the eyes of an animal." full stop is eerier anyway), and Ch 9's opening soldiers paragraph folded to one sentence — its status meta ("I had stopped trying to make them go away") re-ran an arc Chapter Eight had already completed, which was the regression's re-tread complaint. The real/not-real frame stays (it's load-bearing for the ending's vision hedge), and the new memory content — the Bishop's Palace face, the woman's sound, the hospital tents — is untouched.

**One honest process note:** I initially assumed the Ch 9 fold shifted line numbers and moved four anchors; it was intra-line, the checker caught the OK-count drop immediately, and the shifts were reverted in the same commit. The lesson (verify the delta, checker after every anchor touch) is now written into the stack twice, which is probably what it takes.

Manuscript at 31,438 words. The clash was the last genuine fork on the register front — what remains everywhere is taste, shelf, and the things you're deliberately letting ripen.

Both instruments are reading. What I built into each, briefly:

**The demolitionist** is forbidden comparisons outright — every charge must be proven from inside the text — with six hunting orders designed to not overlap hostile v2's: boredom with proof, the mock-aloud party piece, unearned credit (dread borrowed rather than built), the single point of failure, surviving involuntary tics (with counts), and internal rule-breaks. Verdict required: kill or survives-with-damage.

**The Lakota reviewer** is commissioned for exactly what you asked: the strongest *honest* case that the handling is fatal and irremediable — with the honesty enforced structurally. It must engage each defense at full strength (POV discipline, the ending's ownership transfer, period honesty, parasitism-as-subject), and — the key mechanism — it must run every charge through a remediation test: a charge that a feasible revision would fix is serious but not fatal, so an irremediability verdict has to survive its own analysis of what revision could repair. And it's explicitly licensed to return "the fatal case cannot honestly be made" with the strongest sustainable case instead, since an inflated verdict is useless to us. Its persona is a scholar of representation in settler fiction — a beat, not an assigned identity.

Both are drafts-only, blind to the ledgers and to each other. I'll synthesize against the standing representation policy (both cases argued honestly, no text action) and the classroom apparatus's Lakota pairing shelf when they land — the fatal-case verdict in particular bears on the positioning notes' hazard map, whichever way it goes.

<summary>Agent "Lakota-handling fatal case" completed</summary>
<result>PART 1 — THE CASE

The fatal charge against *White Buffalo* is not that it depicts the Lakota badly. It is that the book is built on a formula settler fiction has run for two centuries: the presence of the religion and the absence of the people. The novella requires Lakota belief to be operative — without the sacredness of the white buffalo, Josiah Morrow has no theology, no plot, no death worth three years of walking — and it requires Lakota people to be elsewhere, sitting their horses on ridgelines, so that a white man may conduct his private liturgy with their holiest sign on their own land, uninterrupted, for three years. That double demand is not a flaw of execution that revision could reach. It is the premise.

Consider what the book does with the sacred. It ratifies it through every channel available to a novel that never leaves white heads: the calf's coat is "kept clean by something I do not understand" (04-journal-entry-ii.md:7); she is born out of season and grown beyond nature; a trapper of thirty years has never seen her like, "white to the ground, in November" (07-chapter-four.md:121); Farrell dreams her, precognitively, in a Mexican street before he has ever seen her, with "eyes that were not the eyes of an animal" (15-chapter-eight.md:147). The sacred is functionally real in this book. And every function it performs, it performs for white men. Morrow: "I need to die by something sacred. I need my death to mean something" (06-journal-entry-iii.md:51); "She can give my death a meaning that my life never had" (18-journal-entry-ix.md:53). Farrell, kneeling at the kill site: "For the first time in longer than I could remember, I felt a great and quiet peace" (20-chapter-eleven.md:33) — the same phrase, to the word, that Morrow used on the eve of his death (16-journal-entry-viii.md:95). The book does not merely show a parasite; it distributes the parasite's takings to both narrators. Ptesáŋwiŋ's sign is made a dispensary of white male peace.

Now consider what the book does with the people. The Lakota appear five times. Four times they watch and leave: three riders in January (08-journal-entry-iv.md:51–57), a stake with a red cloth (08:63–66), a village bending its line "the way a river bends around a snag" (12-journal-entry-vi.md:45), watchers "waiting to see what I will do" (16:49–55). The fifth time they receive the buffalo and ride away — and the narrator immediately offers that they may not exist: "Lakota, I thought. Or another vision—another gift from the fever" (20:64). The single scene in which living Lakota act is placed on the same ontological footing as Farrell's hallucinated dead. Meanwhile their three-year forbearance — over an animal whose killing the book itself calls "the greatest of sins" (02-journal-entry-i.md:29), in the years immediately after Blue Water — is explained only by white speculation: "Perhaps they think me mad, and mad men are sacred to them" (02:11); "Perhaps that is why they watch and do not interfere" (16:55). The people are made passive so the parable can proceed.

And the parable is not the white men's own. Two men come before the White Buffalo Woman; the one who approaches grasping is destroyed; the one who forbears receives. Whether by design or convergence, the novella re-runs the encounter structure at the root of the Ptesáŋwiŋ tradition with white principals — Morrow the grasper trampled, Farrell the forbearing witness granted peace — while the tradition's actual inheritors sit at the rim of the valley as scenery. That is not restraint. That is substitution.

The defenses must be met at full strength. POV discipline: the Lakota are never ventriloquized, never given false interiority, never made to explain themselves. True — and beside the point, because the discipline is downstream of the premise. A book that first conscripts a religion as its load-bearing symbol does not earn modesty credit for declining to depict the people it conscripted; the silence is the completion of the taking, not its mitigation. The ending: the buffalo returns to the Lakota, and "No sign passed, of welcome or of warning" (20:65). But the return arrives on cue, at the precise hour of the white climax, choreographed so that the settler reader may watch the inheritance handed back once both white men are done with it — and even their indifference is composed for our benefit, the final service the book asks of them. Period honesty: Harney and Blue Water in white mouths (07:97, 07:157), Spirit Lake in tavern talk (05-chapter-three.md:67–75), "It was Pawnee ground once, and the Sioux took it from them" (09-chapter-five.md:111). Honest, yes — and functioning as alibi, the documentary conscience that lets the deeper structural borrowing pass inspection. Parasitism as subject: the book indicts Morrow — but only for the Hardins. Farrell's judgment is "choices I could not forgive" (19-chapter-ten.md:61), and it attaches to two white settlers murdered in their dooryard. The theology itself — the appropriation of another people's sacred category for a private economy of suicide — is never billed. Morrow calls it "vanity... Pride dressed up as penance" (06:53), and then the book gives him exactly the death he scripted, and gives his witness the peace, and closes. The indictment stops precisely where it would cost the book its consolation.

Why fatal rather than serious? Because these are not scenes; they are members. Remove the sacred and there is no engine. Give the Lakota agency and the three-year vigil collapses. Withhold the peace and both narrators' arcs lose their terminus. Every repair either unmakes the book or adds what its own discipline forbids. A novel whose consolations are minted from a living religion, payable only to white men, cannot be revised into decency; it can only be replaced by a different novel. That is the case for refusing publication in this or any recognizable form.

PART 2 — THE CHARGES, RANKED

**Charge 1: The sacred as settler instrument — the book, not just its villain, converts Ptesáŋwiŋ's sign into white consolation.**
Evidence: Morrow's program is explicit (06-journal-entry-iii.md:51; 14-journal-entry-vii.md:89 "She is sacred. She is more important than I am, more important than the settlers"; 18-journal-entry-ix.md:45–53). The book grants him his scripted death and grants Farrell the identical benediction: "a great and quiet peace" at 16-journal-entry-viii.md:95 and again, verbatim, at 20-chapter-eleven.md:33.
Best defense: The parasitism is the subject. The book prices Morrow's theology at two innocent lives, lets him half-recant ("Pride dressed up as penance," 06:53; the unresolved double apology Farrell reads at 19-chapter-ten.md:57), and then deflates the sacrament at the moment of delivery: "She was not my enemy. She had done what her nature demanded" (20:31) — a wildlife incident, not a consummation. Farrell's "peace" is the peace of a man with nothing left, kneeling in an empty valley, "nothing left to follow" (20:73); the book ends on depletion, not redemption.
Rebuttal: The deflation is available but not enforced. The twinned phrase enforces the opposite reading — that the animal dispenses to the worthy witness what she denied the unworthy suppliant — and the book never once names the appropriation as appropriation. Farrell condemns the murders; no voice, event, or structure condemns the theft of the category. The reader is left holding a sacrament the text quietly honors.
Remediation: Partially fixable, and the fixable part is decisive. The verbatim echo is a phrase-level choice; breaking it — or making Farrell's ending cost him the peace rather than confer it — is compatible with the architecture and with both narrators' arcs. The premise itself (a sacred symbol as engine of a settler story) cannot be revised out, but the premise is not per se profanation: the tradition's own encounter logic — the grasper destroyed — is the very judgment the plot executes on Morrow. What revision can do is stop the book from paying Morrow's dividend to Farrell. Serious; not fatal.

**Charge 2: Plot-subsidized passivity — three years of Lakota non-intervention, explained only by white guesswork.**
Evidence: 02-journal-entry-i.md:11; 08-journal-entry-iv.md:51–57; 16-journal-entry-viii.md:47–55 ("They know what she is... waiting to see what I will do"). The book's own stakes ("To kill one is the greatest of sins," 02:29) make protective action likelier, not less; the people's restraint exists because the parable needs room.
Best defense: The period supplies a real mechanism — "Harney did his work on the Blue Water, and they keep wide of us now" (07-chapter-four.md:157) — and the book plants genuine, unexplained signs of ritual attention from a distance: the peeled stake with the red cloth facing the wintering valley (08:63–66), placed before Morrow ever passed. The final scene shows the watching was never passive: they arrive knowing, and take her home.
Rebuttal: One stake and one arrival cannot carry three years. Avoidance of soldiers and trails does not read across to ceding the tradition's holiest living sign to a white stalker; and both explanations the text offers are a white man's "perhaps." The forbearance is never legible as decision.
Remediation: Clearly fixable within the POV discipline. Two or three more narrator-visible signs on the stake's model — evidence of active, reasoned guardianship at a distance; a wordless encounter; indication that Morrow moves unharmed *because* of what he follows, not despite it — would convert absence into agency without a word of ventriloquism. Fully compatible with the architecture. Not fatal.

**Charge 3: The fever-hedge — the only scene of Lakota action offered as possible hallucination.**
Evidence: "Lakota, I thought. Or another vision—another gift from the fever" (20-chapter-eleven.md:64). Formally, this places living people on the footing of Farrell's dead soldiers and dead Catherine.
Best defense: Epistemic honesty. Farrell has been unreliable for five chapters and knows it (17-chapter-nine.md:3); a narrator who suddenly claimed certainty here would break the book. And the scene's physics resist the hedge — the buffalo "fell in among them, and together they moved north" (20:67); his visions have never moved matter.
Rebuttal: The defense explains the hedge; it does not price it. Given how little page-space the Lakota receive, spending one of their two sentences of presence on the possibility that they are a symptom is a cost the book seems not to have noticed it was paying.
Remediation: Trivially fixable — cut or invert the hedge, or anchor the riders in narrator-independent trace (tracks, the herd's reaction, the horse's). One sentence. Not fatal; barely even structural.

**Charge 4: Borrowed authority — the religion cited as authentication, rendered only as hearsay and settler cliché.**
Evidence: "The Indians hold them sacred... a white buffalo calf woman brought them their most holy things, their pipe, their rituals" (02:29) — trader's talk, and the sum total of doctrinal content in the book. "Mad men are sacred to them" (02:11). "The Indians are right to revere her" (12-journal-entry-vi.md:75). "I hope they take her back to whatever sacred place she came from" (18:65).
Best defense: This is what an 1856 Virginian drifter would know, sourced as what it is — posts-on-the-Missouri hearsay — and the alternative (staging ceremony, naming Ptesáŋwiŋ, doctrinal exposition through white mouths) would be far worse. The text even corrects its own cliché: Morrow imagines a "sacred place"; the ending gives her *people*.
Rebuttal: The defense holds for register but not for function. The hearsay is deployed to do real load-bearing work — it is the entire warrant for the book's stakes — and a book may choose its characters' every word. "Mad men are sacred to them" is a settler trope the novel repeats without friction.
Remediation: Fixable by subtraction and precision, which is the only safe direction: prune the trope at 02:11 (or give it friction), keep the hearsay framing, add nothing doctrinal. Compatible with the architecture; indeed demanded by it. Not fatal.

**Charge 5: Period atrocity discourse in white mouths (Blue Water, Spirit Lake, Pawnee dispossession).**
Evidence: 07:97–99 (Farrell's "When are they not restless?"), 07:157, 05:67–75, 09:111.
Best defense: This is the book working. Harney is named as perpetrator by a white rider who adds "I don't fault them"; Spirit Lake circulates as tavern fear-talk, marked as such; the trader's Pawnee line refuses the Eden myth; Hardin's speculative logic (09:71–73) shows settlement as invasion-by-arithmetic.
Rebuttal: None of substance. I concede the charge fails.
Remediation: None needed.

PART 3 — THE VERDICT

**(b) SERIOUS BUT REMEDIABLE.**

I made my best effort at (a), and I state explicitly: the fatal case cannot be honestly sustained. Its irremediability claim fails on inspection. Charges 2, 3, and 4 are repairable by ordinary revision — in two cases by sentences, not scenes — and all repairs run *with* the book's architecture, not against it. Charge 1 is the real matter, and even there the load-bearing defect is a distributable one: the book's error is not that a broken white man builds a suicide theology out of another people's sacred category — the novel visibly prices that theology at two murdered innocents and executes on its author the tradition's own judgment of the grasping man — but that the book then pays the dead man's dividend to its surviving narrator in the dead man's exact words. That is a choice, and choices can be unmade. The residue that no revision removes — a settler novel drawing narrative power from a living religion it declines, rightly, to depict — is a genuine cost, but a cost is not a verdict. To call the premise unpublishable per se, I would have to hold that no settler fiction may be built around a sacred symbol of a living tradition under any treatment, a categorical rule this text does not license me to derive, least of all when its final image is the sacred removed from white custody, white use, and white sight, by people who owe the kneeling white man nothing and give him exactly that.

The required repairs, without which the current form should not go to press:

1. Break the twinned benediction. "A great and quiet peace" cannot appear at both 16-journal-entry-viii.md:95 and 20-chapter-eleven.md:33. Farrell must not receive, verbatim, the sacrament his subject died grasping for. Whatever Farrell is left with at the end, it should cost more and console less.
2. Cut or invert the fever-hedge at 20:64, or ground the riders in physical fact his visions never leave. The Lakota's one act in this book must not be optional.
3. Convert forbearance into decision. Two or three additional narrator-visible signs of active guardianship across the three years — the stake at 08:63 is the model and proof the book knows how — so that the non-intervention reads as Lakota reasoning observed from outside, not authorial convenience.
4. Prune the settler trope at 02:11 or give it friction in Morrow's own hand.
5. Process, not text: this book should be read by Lakota readers before publication, and carry a brief note situating the white buffalo in living religious practice and naming the hearsay rendering as deliberate. The front matter's disclosure that the prose was machine-generated raises this obligation; there was no human author's lived attention standing behind these choices, so it must be supplied now, from outside.

What the repaired book can then honestly carry, it should carry without apology: the POV discipline is real and is the book's best ethical feature — no ventriloquism, no staged ceremony, no interiority invented for people the narrators cannot know; the period texture around Blue Water is better than most literary Westerns manage; and the ending's withheld sign — "No sign passed, of welcome or of warning" — is, once the surrounding repairs stop the book from cashing it as absolution, the right silence, earned.</result>
<usage><subagent_tokens>77206</subagent_tokens><tool_uses>22</tool_uses><duration_ms>350756</duration_ms></usage>
</task-notification>

## ASSISTANT

The Lakota verdict is in, and it's the most valuable document any instrument has produced for this book. Interim relay while the demolitionist finishes:

**The verdict: (b) SERIOUS BUT REMEDIABLE — with the fatal case explicitly attempted and explicitly declared unsustainable.** The irremediability claim failed its own remediation test: three of the five charges are repairable "by sentences, not scenes," and the reviewer concedes the strongest defense outright — the final image is "the sacred removed from white custody, white use, and white sight, by people who owe the kneeling white man nothing and give him exactly that." Charge 5 (period atrocity discourse) it withdrew entirely: "this is the book working."

The case it *could* sustain is sharp and mostly new to us in formulation: the book's real error isn't Morrow's parasitism (which the text prices at two murders and punishes with the tradition's own grasper-destroyed logic) — it's that **the book pays the dead man's dividend to the surviving narrator in the dead man's exact words.** The twinned "great and quiet peace" is, on this reading, the mechanism by which the appropriation stops being the villain's and becomes the book's.

**Its five required repairs create two direct collisions with standing rulings, which are now yours to adjudicate:**
1. *Break the twinned benediction* — this is #36 escalated from taste to requirement (the tally against the unmarked peace is now effectively 4–2, and this instrument argues it on representational rather than craft grounds — the strongest version of the charge yet). It also converges exactly with your anti-consolation direction: "Whatever Farrell is left with at the end, it should cost more and console less." Another entry in #29's file.
2. *The fever-hedge at 20:63* — the period auditor ruled it LOAD-BEARING ("never trim it"); this reviewer says it makes the Lakota's one act in the book optional. But the reviewer itself supplied the reconciliation: keep the hedge, ground the riders in physical fact "his visions never leave" — and there's a one-clause version available, since five chapters of ghosts have never once moved Farrell's horse.
3. *Convert forbearance into decision* — two or three more narrator-visible guardianship signs on the stake's model, POV-clean. This is an actual expansion proposal, the first instrument to ask for *additions* on the Lakota axis.
4. *The "mad men are sacred to them" trope at 02:11* — prune or give friction. Small.
5. *Process, not text:* Lakota readers before publication, plus a situating note — with a genuinely arresting argument tied to our front matter: the disclosure that the prose was machine-generated "raises this obligation; there was no human author's lived attention standing behind these choices, so it must be supplied now, from outside." That belongs in the #38 attribution conversation and the positioning hazard map.

No text touched — the collisions are yours, and the demolitionist's report should land before we decide anything. Full synthesis and stack logging when it does.

<summary>Agent "Hostile non-comparatist demolition" completed</summary>
<result>PART 1 — THE DEMOLITION

*White Buffalo* is two books in a locked room, and each one quietly strangles the other.

The first book is a procedural: a hired man named Farrell rides west through named towns, real prices, ferry crossings, frostbite protocol. This book is good. It knows that a wet boot kills, that whiskey is a supply line, that a sweaty horse in winter is a dying horse. Its opening chapter — an old merchant in his second-best coat, watch left at the boarding house, bills counted three times — earns everything it touches.

The second book is a scripture: a Mexican War veteran's journal of following a sacred white buffalo for three years so she can grow big enough to kill him. This book asks for faith, and it makes the fatal error of asking in front of the first book. The procedural half trains the reader to demand evidence — and then the mystical half offers a buffalo whose sacredness rests entirely on hearsay the text itself labels "trader's talk," a coat "kept clean by something I do not understand" that the very next season is "darkened by the wet," and a growth rate the diarist himself concedes is impossible before waving it off: "no cow reaches her size in three summers, but she was born out of season and has been early in everything since." The book teaches you to check its math, then begs you not to.

The structure defeats its own suspense twice over. The journal, interleaved from page one, tells us who killed the Hardins a full chapter before Farrell deduces it; his detective work in the homestead — the best sustained writing in the novel — solves a mystery the reader has already been handed. Meanwhile the middle journals go inert for a fifth of the book, because the diarist's single desire is deferred by fiat: "But she is not ready, and so I am not ready, and so we both endure." Entries can only re-attest status. She is white. She has grown. I wait. He discovers "She is beautiful" in October 1856 and discovers it again, nearly verbatim, in May 1857. Nine journal sections end on the same held note — wait, ready, soon, now — a cadence so regular the reader could write the next entry unassisted.

And then the book elides the only event it ever promised. Every witness testimony, every tracked footprint, every day of Farrell's withdrawal-wracked pursuit is suspense machinery pointed at one rendezvous: man meets buffalo. It happens between chapters. Farrell arrives a day late to the climax; so does the reader. Both murders happen offstage too — the journal entry describing the killing of the Hardins exists (the diarist says so; Farrell quotes it) but is silently withheld from the alternating structure that presents itself as the complete journal. A book may practice restraint. It may not run a suspense engine for two hundred pages and then decline to stage the payoff while letting one character read pages the reader is denied. That is not economy. That is a dodged check.

The ending then pays itself in counterfeit. Farrell, who never met the dead man and did nothing but arrive late and read, feels "a great and quiet peace" — the exact phrase, word for word, that the diarist used three days earlier in his own final pages. Two narrators, one voice, one groomed cadence. The murdered Catherine Hardin, hallucinated by a man who never knew her, stands over her killer's corpse with "no fear in her" — the victim conscripted, through her hired avenger's fever, to bless the tableau of her murderer's transfiguration. And the Lakota riders, who never speak in the entire novel, arrive to escort the buffalo offstage: another people's religion, known to this text only as campfire rumor, spent at the close as metaphysical fact.

Underneath, the seams show. The diarist has killed "seventeen men with certainty" in June 1856 and "dozens, certainly" in a single afternoon by his final entry. The herd migrates south for winter when the theme needs endurance and north through January when the plot needs a valley. Farrell nearly dies of one wet boot; the diarist sleeps buried in snow under one blanket, loses feeling in hands and feet for weeks, and keeps every finger for three more seasons.

Verdict: survives, with damage — and the damage is structural, not cosmetic. The opening chapter, the homestead search with Catherine's crossed-out letter drafts, and the material honesty of the trail are the work of a real book. But the middle third must stop idling, the two voices must be pried apart, and the climax must either be staged or the machinery that promises it dismantled. As it stands, this is a fine procedural welded to an altar it cannot make the reader kneel at.

PART 2 — THE CHARGE SHEET

**1. Boredom, with proof.**
The stretch is Journal IV through Journal VI (08, 10, 12) — roughly a fifth of the text. Mechanism: the diarist's one desire (death by buffalo) is deferred by authorial fiat, so entries can only restate status, and they do, in the same words:
- "She endures. The herd endures. I endure because I must." (08:21)
- "The herd is still moving. She is still with them." (08:31)
- "She is with them, and I am with her." (10:75); "She is with them. She is always with them." (16:7)
- "She is beautiful." (06:59) rediscovered as "She is beautiful in a way I did not expect." (10:21) — same epiphany, seven months apart.
- An entire dated entry (12:19–23, September the Twenty-Ninth) contains no event at all: the settlers are still there; winter is coming.
The chapters have their own idle: the witness-testimony scene is run four times with identical beats — sighting, "thin as a rail," the eyes, the shudder (05:109–122; 07:113–126; 07:171–181; 09:89–101 in file 09). By the second, the reader can recite the third.

**2. The party piece.**
Journal VI, November 19 (12:69–77): "She is the most beautiful thing I have ever seen. I do not say this lightly. I saw a woman's face by candlelight once, in a time before the war. It does not compare." A starving man solemnly ranking a buffalo above the only woman he ever loved. Runner-up, read in sequence for full effect: "I said: I am waiting for you. She raised her head and looked at me." (04:59–61) — a man declaiming courtship-cadence vows across open grass to a calf. The mechanism of mockability: total earnestness plus the vocabulary of romance aimed at livestock, with no narrative frame aware of the hazard.

**3. Unearned credit.**
- "a great and quiet peace" (16:95) recycled verbatim as Farrell's closing emotion (20:33) — peace installed by cadence, not by anything Farrell did; he arrived late and read a book.
- Catherine's ghost blessing the death-scene: "She was watching the white buffalo, and there was no fear in her." (20:41) The victim, hallucinated by a stranger, absolves the composition.
- The Lakota as sacredness-certifiers: watchers on ridges (08:51), a stake with red cloth (08:63), silent escort at the close (20:61–67). They never speak; their religion, sourced in-text only as "trader's talk" (02:29), is spent as fact.
- Dread by testimony: four witnesses assert terror ("there was nothing in his eyes. Nothing at all." 07:179) about a man the reader has inhabited since page two and knows to be a tired suicide, not a demon. The dread is borrowed against access the structure already gave away.
- "She can give my death a meaning that my life never had." (16 via 19:55; 18:53) Meaning asserted, never demonstrated; the ending endorses it by staging rather than testing it.
- "You should prepare yourself, Mr. Aldridge... No. You haven't." (01:167–171) — portent issued before any content exists to back it.

**4. The single point of failure.**
The buffalo's sacredness. Every load path runs through it: Morrow's plan is tragedy only if she is numinous (otherwise he is a lunatic who murdered two settlers to protect a cow); Farrell's peace, Catherine's calm ghost, and the Lakota escort all presume it. The book shores it with assertion only: the impossible-growth handwave (16:17), the self-cleaning coat (04:7) — revoked at 06:47 ("her white coat darkened by the wet") and 20:21 (blood crusted) — herd deference, and other people's unvoiced reverence. She herself never does anything but stand, walk, and trample offstage. Likelihood of refusal: high, because the book's own procedural half trains the reader to audit claims. A reader who sees an albino cow is left holding a squalid double murder and a suicide, with the narrative's full moral weight resting on air. Morrow's charmed three-winter survival (one blanket, no gun, dead extremities, 08:73) is part of the same purchase.

**5. Surviving tics.**
- Section-closing cadence: all 9 journal sections end on the wait/ready/soon note (04:67; 06:67; 08:101; 10:91; 12:99; 14:117; 16:101; 18:81; 02:67). Chapters likewise groomed: "I did not look back." (05:135), "But I kept riding." (17:95), "I followed them both." (13:115).
- Negation-restatement sentence shape: "I did not" x61 across 2,245 lines; "She did not" x14; the ghost-dialogue beat recurs identically — "They did not answer. They never answered." (15:33); "She did not answer. She only walked." (17:18); "She did not answer. But she turned" (15:151).
- Numbness declared rather than dramatized, 6 instances: "I felt nothing" / "did not feel anything" (06:37; 10:73; 12:59; 18:33; 18:51; 19:45).
- "for a long time" x10; "sacred" x16; "perhaps" x17; "something" x79.
- Cross-narrator and cross-character phrase recycling: "like a ship through water" (06:9, Morrow) reappears in Farrell's own narration (20:57); "like a dog following its master" (05:117) / "like a dog that's lost its master" (09:89) from two different speakers; "thin as a rail, beard down to/past his chest" verbatim from two independent witnesses (05:121; 07:175). The tics do not respect character boundaries — that is where the craft stops being choice.

**6. Internal breaks.**
- Kill-count contradiction: "I killed seventeen men with certainty" (04:41), "the man who killed seventeen men at Monterrey and Buena Vista" (10:87), vs. Buena Vista alone: "I do not know how many. Dozens, certainly." (18:33).
- Two service records: Journal IX puts him in Davis's Mississippians at Buena Vista under Taylor (18:27–37); Journal II marches him to Mexico City ("By Mexico City I had forgotten..." 04:63). The text's own two accounts cannot both hold.
- The coat miracle revoked when inconvenient: 04:7 vs. 06:47 vs. 20:21.
- Herd ecology reversed for plot: the book establishes south-in-winter, stationary-in-deep-snow (08:19, 08:39, 12:89–91); yet December tracks lead north from the homestead (13:63–65), the pair crosses the Loup Fork southland in early December (07:175), and by January 1 the herd is settled north of the river (16:75).
- The homestead's winter trackway is one buffalo plus one man (13:63: "Two sets.") while the journal insists she is never apart from the herd (16:7) and leads it (14:33). A hundred animals leave no third story in the snow.
- Physics double standard: Farrell nearly dies of one wet boot with fire, horse, and supplies (07:23–37); Morrow survives three winters with a blanket and a knife, unfelt hands and feet for weeks (08:73), intact.
- Withdrawal runs on theme-time: the text itself distinguishes real DT visions ("the snakes on the walls," 15:25) from these curated, punctual ghosts, which persist for weeks past their own precedent and turn benign exactly at the ending (20:39).
- Knowledge the narrator shouldn't have: "His name was Josiah Morrow." (19:37) — the journal as shown to the reader never contains a name.
- The elided entry: "I have written of what was done in it, and I will not write of it again" (16:5); Farrell quotes that entry (19:47). It exists; the reader, presented with the journal in sequence throughout, is never shown it. The book's information economy is breached in its own favor.
- Small but telling: Farrell attributes "Like a ship through water" to the first-sight entry (19:41); the first entry says "like a ghost" (02:27) — the ship is from September (06:9). The book misquotes its own central document. And Journal I claims "I have lost count of the months" (02:5) under a precise date heading, a contradiction patched only eighteen months later (12:33).

PART 3 — WHAT STANDS

Chapter One stands whole: the second-best coat, the counted bills, the hat left on the table are exact and earned. The homestead search in Chapter Six — the crossed-out letter draft about children, the frozen ink, the almanac margins full of figuring — is the best writing in the book and would survive any review. The material spine throughout (whiskey as logistics, ice, horses, prices) is honest work that never once cheats. The two-narrator engine is a genuine design; it is the execution — collapsed voices, spoiled suspense, elided climax — that fails it, not the idea.</result>

Options: **(a) cut** Farrell's sentence — 20:33 becomes "I sat back on my heels and looked at her. The cheap instrument is the horse: five chapters of ghosts have never once moved him. One clause where the riders appear — e.g., after "They came from the north, three or four of them, on the ridge": *"The horse lifted his head to watch them, the first notice he had taken of anything I saw."* — the hedge stays sayable, but the reader holds proof Farrell doesn't.

**3. Guardianship signs.** The first instrument ever to request Lakota-axis *additions*: two or three narrator-visible signs across the three years, on the stake's model, converting forbearance into legible decision — including the idea that Morrow moves unharmed *because* of what he follows. I do not know if it is true, or whose it is."). Prune is subtraction; friction marks the hearsay as hearsay in Morrow's own hand.

- **The horse grounds the riders.** After the hedge: *"Behind me the horse shifted, his ears gone forward toward the ridge. He had never once taken notice of the soldiers."* The hedge stays sayable (period auditor satisfied), and the reader now holds proof Farrell doesn't — the two rulings reconciled in two sentences.
- **The 02:11 trope is gone, by way of your mapping question.** Working out the ontology first made the fix obvious: Morrow treats madness as a *verdict of observers*, always community-indexed — "I know how this will sound" to journal-readers, "they will probably think I was mad" of white finders — while his own operative categories are empty/filled, weapon/man, clean/unclean. His final position is pragmatist: "It does not matter. I believe it, and that is enough." So the old line failed twice: it projected a settler trope onto the Lakota *and* had Morrow confidently modeling categories he has no access to. The honest-to-Morrow line is epistemic humility: **"I do not know what they take me for."** He goes on guessing freely about his own community's verdicts (18:65 untouched) — those are the only ones he can presume to use. The full mapping is in the character note as §7.

**Two proposals awaiting you:**
1. **The non-sacral twin.** To preserve the contamination idea after paying the price of the words: Morrow's *"I am tired. I am so tired."* (18:51) surfacing tense-shifted in Farrell at the kneeling beat — *"I was tired. I was so tired."* Exhaustion instead of benediction: it does the arrived-at-his-coordinates work, rereaders catch the verbatim, and it costs more and consoles less — arguably the true thing under Morrow's theology all along. (Alternative: leave the beat bare as now cut, with ship+illumination carrying the device alone.)
2. **Guardianship signs, two candidates** (repair 3): **(b)** JE VIII — Morrow finds the ridge stake *renewed*: the old one drawn out and lying in the grass, a new peeled stake, bright cloth. "They have not forgotten her. They are keeping her." Guardianship as maintained practice, and Morrow quietly relativized inside someone else's watch. **(c)** Farrell-side, Ch 8 or 9 — unshod pony tracks running with the trail on the high ground, days old: the pursuit was never unobserved. A bolder third exists (the death-meadow ridge already trodden before the riders appear — the death happened under their watch the whole time), flagged but riskier: it reassigns the ending's witness in a way you'd want to sit with.

Pick any, all, or none — (b) and (c) are compatible and together would convert three years of forbearance into visible, reasoned attention from both narrators' sides.

That last one is where I think your "bonus pre-existing issues" hunch pays off. Three standing problems the guardianship signs could close at once:

1. **The fortune on the hoof.** The trapper — a thirty-year hide-man — has *seen* her, and the rumor chain runs down to Council Bluffs. A white robe was legendarily valuable, yet no white hunter goes after her in three years. Currently unexplained. One clause in the existing rumor-chain dialogue ("that's Sioux ground, and they keep it"-shaped) plus visible guardianship would close it — the Lakota presence becomes the reason the book's own commercial world doesn't devour its center.
2. **Morrow's charmed passage** — three years unharmed on unceded land, which the demolitionist filed under the sacredness purchase. The reviewer's own formulation ("unharmed *because* of what he follows, not despite it") converts the implausibility into legible tolerance — watched, catalogued, suffered to walk.
3. **The herd's untouched winters** — a two-hundred-head band wintering within reach of villages, never hunted on the page. Guardianship of *her* implies managed distance from the herd she leads.

All three are currently carried as mythic license; the signs would move them onto the ledger of observed decisions — which is exactly the reviewer's repair, converting forbearance into reasoning visible from outside. When the research lands I'll draft the candidates — the JE VIII stake renewal, the Farrell-side flanking tracks, the rumor-chain clause — against whatever the record actually attests, and bring them as a package with citations.

<result>## Research findings: Lakota signs of presence and guardianship, 1850s Nebraska Territory

All primary texts below were verified against full digitized editions (Gutenberg/Internet Archive); quotes are verbatim.

---

### 1. MATERIAL SIGNS OF PRESENCE / OFFERING / ATTENTION

**a. Buffalo skulls with scarlet cloth tied to the horns + "medicine poles" with cloth streamers, on a lookout butte.** Henry A. Boller (opposition trader, Upper Missouri 1858–62 — exactly your years) describes climbing a butte that was "a famous lookout, commanding the prairies for many miles in every direction": "On the very summit were placed a couple of buffalo-skulls, with pieces of scarlet cloth fastened around each horn. Two medicine poles were also set up, with pieces of calico flying from them, gifts to propitiate the Great Spirit, that he would send them plenty of buffalo." — Boller, *Among the Indians: Eight Years in the Far West, 1858–1866* (1868), ch. on the winter camp. Note the explicit purpose: **offerings placed on high ground specifically to draw and keep buffalo** — the closest attested thing to material "guardianship of the herd."

**b. Painted cairn surmounted by a buffalo skull and arrow bundle — and its destruction as readable news.** Boller again, near Fort Atkinson: "we passed by a pile of stones painted with vermilion, and surmounted by a bundle of arrows and a buffalo-skull, which had been erected by the Assinniboines as a landmark." Returning the same day: "The stones were scattered about, the skull thrown to one side, and the arrows broken and strewn around, showing conclusively that a war-party had passed by, and narrowly missed us." (*Among the Indians*, 1868.) A sign *and* a sign-event: hostile passers-by deliberately desecrate another nation's marker, and a traveler reads the change. Directly transferable to your narrator.

**c. White "medicine flags" painted with sun and moon, attended by a fasting man.** Boller: "two white medicine flags were flying on the prairie, each bearing a rude painting in vermilion of the sun and moon, to which they were offerings. An Indian walked in a circle around them all day long, crying and praying... Near him was a small pile of human skulls, around each of which was bound a strip of scarlet cloth. His lance, thrust into the ground beside them, supported his shield and medicine bag." (ibid.) The skull-circle element is Mandan/Hidatsa (village-tribe practice), but the cloth flags/vermilion sun are pan-Plains.

**d. A white calfskin hung on a hilltop as a sun-offering — stolen by a white man.** Rudolph Kurz, near Fort Union (Denig's post), September 1851: "The Spaniard took advantage of the darkness to filch from a neighboring hill a tanned white calfskin that had been hung there by an Indian as a votive offering to the sun." — *Journal of Rudolph Friederich Kurz, 1846–1852*, ed. Hewitt, BAE Bulletin 115 (1937). This single sentence attests: (1) white skins hung on high ground as sun offerings, visible to travelers; (2) white men casually looting such offerings. It is almost your novel's premise in miniature.

**e. Valued articles carried alone into the hills, deliberately damaged, and left.** Edwin Denig (written c. 1854 at Fort Union): "The Indian takes some article of value alone into the hills or woods, lights the pipe, and invokes the aid of Wakoñda... This article is then damaged or destroyed and left there." He adds the article "must be something of value, must have caused the Indian some trouble or expense to procure" — one man paid six buffalo robes for three trivial objects he had vowed to sacrifice. Also: after the annual Sacred Lodge, offerings (skins, cloth, kettles, beads) are "destroyed in such manner as to be of no use to anyone who finds them, and hung on different trees or bushes in the neighborhood." — Denig, *Indian Tribes of the Upper Missouri*, ed. Hewitt, 46th BAE Annual Report (Gutenberg #49557). Key outsider-observable detail: **offerings found in the landscape are deliberately ruined** — a slashed kettle or torn cloth hanging in a bush is a sacrifice, not trash. A tracker who knows this reads intention; a greenhorn reads litter.

**f. Scaffold burials with buffalo-skull circles.** Parkman, at Fort Laramie, 1846: "a cluster of strange objects like scaffolds rising in the distance against the red western sky. They bore aloft some singular looking burdens; and at their foot glimmered something white like bones... The white objects upon the ground were buffalo skulls, arranged in the mystic circle commonly seen at Indian places of sepulture upon the prairie." He adds that Dakota placed dead chiefs near the fort so enemies wouldn't desecrate them — and that Crow war parties had recently "thrown the bodies from the scaffolds." — Parkman, *The Oregon Trail* (1849), ch. "The Frontier"/Fort Laramie chapters (Gutenberg #1015). Kurz gives the construction close-up (Fort Berthold, 1851): "a scaffold was constructed of four stakes held together with crossbeams... wrapped in his robe... beneath the covering of a new red blanket. His medicine pouch was fastened to one of the posts." (BAE Bull. 115.)

**g. Marked/carved trees.** Parkman, camped on Laramie Creek: a cottonwood "full six feet in diameter; on one side it was marked by a party of Indians with various inexplicable hieroglyphics, commemorating some warlike enterprise, and aloft among the branches were the remains of a scaffolding, where dead bodies had once been deposited." (*The Oregon Trail*.) Maximilian of Wied (1833–34) noted burial trees with trunks painted red among the Assiniboine (*Travels in the Interior of North America*, vol. I).

**h. Guardianship of buffalo asserted against whites — attested in your exact place and years.** G. K. Warren's own 1857 report: near Bear Butte, Hunkpapa/Miniconjou bands stopped his survey party because they were holding buffalo herds nearby and would not have them disturbed; Warren "consented to wait three days without advancing" and negotiated with Bear's Rib. — Warren, *Preliminary Report of Explorations in Nebraska and Dakota, 1855–'56–'57*. Warren explicitly likened driving off the buffalo to an attack on their herds of cattle. This is documentary warrant for Lakota actively *policing* white access to buffalo in 1857.

---

### 2. TRACKING / SIGN VOCABULARY

All from Capt. Randolph B. Marcy, *The Prairie Traveler* (1859 — publication-year perfect), ch. V–VI (Gutenberg #23066), verbatim:

- **War party vs. village on the move:** "A party of Indians... starting out upon a war excursion, leave their families behind, and never transport their lodges... If, therefore, an Indian trail is discovered with the marks of the lodge-poles upon it, it has certainly not been made by a war-party; but if the track do not show the trace of lodge-poles, it will be equally certain that a war or hunting party has passed." (Lodge-pole drag = travois/pole-drag furrows flanking the hoofprints.)
- **Shod vs. unshod:** "It is not a difficult matter to distinguish the tracks of American horses from those of Indian horses, as the latter are never shod; moreover, they are much smaller."
- **Mounted party vs. wild herd:** dung "in a single pile" = mustangs stopping to stale; "a party of Indians would keep their horses in motion, and the ordure would be scattered along the road." In timber, loose mustangs pass under limbs "too low to admit the passage of a man on horseback."
- **Aging a trail:** Marcy's Delaware guide dated a trail to "two days before, in the morning" from sand crusted onto pressed-down grass spears — dried on while dew-wet, and no dew had fallen since. Also: fresh tracks show moisture in turned earth; in loose sand, very fresh tracks still have sand "running back into" them; rain "washes off all the sharp edges," so tracks date relative to the last rain; trodden grass bent in the direction of travel shows "a different shade of green" when sighted along from behind, long after it stands back up.
- **Gait and evasion:** track spacing reveals walk/trot/gallop (Marcy prints a diagram); pursued Indians "scatter as much as possible" to a prearranged rendezvous, so any single track leads there.

Boller (1858–62) supplies the corroborating field habit: hunters sweeping country with glasses and inferring hostiles from unaccountably wild buffalo (spooked game as sign of hidden men) — the cairn episode above.

---

### 3. WHITE BUFFALO: WHAT IS ACTUALLY ATTESTED

**The attested complex centers on the taken robe, not on a guarded living animal.**

- **Catlin, 1832 (Mandan), *Letters and Notes*, Letter 19:** "A white buffalo robe is a great curiosity, even in the country of buffaloes... there is not one in an hundred thousand, perhaps, that is white." Mandans sent eight men with eight horses plus goods 200 miles to buy a white robe from Blackfeet; it was "the most costly article of traffic in these regions," was "consecrated by the doctors," hoisted on a long pole over the medicine lodge, and left "as an offering to the Great Spirit, until it decays and falls to the ground" — alongside other cloth sacrifices.
- **Maximilian of Wied, 1833–34 (Mandan), *Travels*, vol. II:** the Mandans "set a high value on the hide of a white buffalo cow, for which they often give fifteen horses, guns, cloth, blankets, robes"; the hide was suspended aloft as a sun offering and left to rot. (Widely quoted via David Dary, *The Buffalo Book*, 1974.)
- **Kurz, July 1851 (Fort Berthold):** "One of the Metisse brought a white buffalo robe to sell and received two good racehorses in exchange. Such a skin is very valuable, for white or dappled buffaloes are very rare." — attested open-market trade of a white robe in your decade.
- **Lakota-specific, near-period: Alice C. Fletcher, "The White Buffalo Festival of the Uncpapas," 16th Annual Report, Peabody Museum (1884), pp. 260–75** (fieldwork Standing Rock, 1882). Attested: the white buffalo "generally remains near the centre of the herd, which makes it difficult of approach... considered as the chief or sacred one of the herd." Footnote: "The Yankton, Teton, Santee, and Sissiton Indians consider it a grave responsibility to kill a white buffalo, and it is almost feared, on account of the implied obligation to fulfil the rites pertaining to it." The Hunkpapa rites: hide tanned by a paid, ceremonially dressed **virgin** while men watch "that the skin may not be cut or broken"; hide never touched by hand once dressed (turned with poles); a priest paints concentric red circles between the shoulders; a pole trimmed and painted in four red stripes mixed with fat from the white buffalo's heart, carried on a litter; ceremony in a 30–40-ft brush circle with a **buffalo skull set on bared, mellowed earth, sage thrust into both eye sockets and mouth** (same treatment as the sun-dance skull). Also economic: a hide "recently exchanged for three horses and a mule (equal to between one and two hundred dollars)"; **"The news of the capture of a white buffalo soon spread beyond the limits of a tribe, and proposals for its purchase are often received from remote points. Sometimes promises to surrender it are exacted beforehand."**
- **What is NOT attested:** any formal practice of protecting, herding, or ceremonially attending a *living* white buffalo. Hornaday (*The Extermination of the American Bison*, 1889): albinism "of extremely rare occurrence"; "not over ten or eleven white buffaloes, or white buffalo skins, were ever seen by white men"; no albino "so far as I can learn, ever had the good fortune to attain adult size," its conspicuousness drawing "an unusual number of enemies." The attested reasons a band might *watch but not kill* one are: it stays deep in the herd (hard to approach), and killing it triggers feared, expensive ritual obligations that not everyone can afford (Fletcher). Guardianship-of-the-living-animal must be built from those two attested facts plus the attested guardianship of herds generally (Warren 1857) — anything more ceremonial than that is invention.
- The oft-repeated claim that **Denig** prices white robes: I could not verify it in his Assiniboine monograph (full-text searched; no white-buffalo passage), and I could not access *Five Indian Tribes* full text. Treat "Denig on white robes" as unverified; attribute horse-price figures to Maximilian, Catlin, Kurz, and Fletcher, which are solid.

---

### 4. THE COMMERCIAL THREAT, LATE 1850s

- **Scale of the robe trade in your decade:** Catlin (1832) already estimated 150,000–200,000 robes/year drawn from the region; NPS histories put Fort Union's 1850s peak at ~150,000 robes/year. The middle Missouri robe trade was fully industrialized before the 1870s hide era — plenty of traders and engagés in range of the Loup/Niobrara.
- **Baseline prices (Denig's own Fort Union ledger, 1851–52, reproduced in his monograph):** a buffalo robe carried "an imaginary value of $3 each in the country"; 1 horse = 10 robes; ¾ yard scarlet cloth = 1 robe. So an ordinary robe ≈ $3; a white robe trading at "two good racehorses" (Kurz, 1851) ≈ 20+ robes' value, and Maximilian's fifteen-horses figure ≈ 150 robes' value — a fortune by country reckoning.
- **What whites later paid, showing the ceiling:** Hornaday: a "buckskin robe" (so-called white buffalo) sold in Miles City, 1882, for **$200 against $3.50 for ordinary robes**; "Prairie Dog Dave" Morrow's white buffalo brought **$1,000 cash** from Robert Wright at Dodge City, 1873; a Texas Ranger company's white hide reportedly brought $500 in St. Louis, 1875; J. Wright Mooar kept his 1876 white hide against large offers (Dary, *The Buffalo Book*). All post-1859, but they establish that any white man who understood the market would see three figures where he normally saw three dollars.
- **Attested white appropriation in the 1850s itself:** Kurz's "Spaniard" stealing the votive white calfskin off the hill (1851), under cover of dark, on the ride home from an ordinary meat hunt — casual, opportunistic, unpunished. And Fletcher attests purchase offers traveling "from remote points" with promises "exacted beforehand" — meaning word of a white animal moved faster than the animal. A known living white buffalo drawing hunters is therefore a modest extrapolation, not attested as an event; what IS attested is the demand side, the price gradient, and the theft of white-hide offerings.

---

### USABLE SIGNS (period-safe, observed-from-outside)

- **Buffalo skulls on a butte summit with scarlet cloth tied around each horn, beside peeled poles flying calico strips** — Boller, 1858–62; explicitly to bring/keep buffalo. Your strongest single sign; pairs naturally with your existing red-cloth stake.
- **A vermilion-painted rock cairn topped with a buffalo skull and a bundle of arrows — later found scattered and broken**, telling the narrator a hostile party passed (Boller). Sign that changes state.
- **A deliberately ruined valuable left in the hills** (slashed kettle, torn blanket, damaged gun) with pipe-ash nearby — Denig; the ruin is the point, and a narrator/companion can misread it as trash before a plainsman corrects him. No Lakota speech needed.
- **A tanned white calfskin hung on a hilltop as a sun offering** — Kurz, 1851; direct thematic rhyme with the white buffalo, and attested as something a white man might steal.
- **Scaffold burials sighted at distance against the sky, buffalo skulls "arranged in the mystic circle" at their feet; red blanket wrappings; medicine pouch tied to a post** — Parkman 1846, Kurz 1851.
- **Trail vocabulary:** lodge-pole drag marks = families (safe); no pole marks = war/hunting party (danger); unshod, smaller pony tracks; scattered vs. piled dung; sand-crusted grass spears dating a trail to a dewy morning — all Marcy, 1859, quotable nearly verbatim.
- **Flagged as extrapolation:** Lakota riders *watching* or escorting a white buffalo, warning whites off it. Build it from Warren 1857 (bands physically halting a white party to protect buffalo herds) + Fletcher (the animal is "the chief of the herd," killing it a feared obligation, its hide spoken for from afar). Plausible and defensible — but the specific practice of attending a living white animal is not in the record; keep it behavioral (pickets, watchers, a warning sign like your stake), not ceremonial.
- **Avoid inventing:** any ritual around the living animal; any Lakota explanation voiced to whites; "Denig says" for white-robe values (unverified — use Maximilian/Catlin/Kurz/Fletcher instead).

Sources: [Marcy, *The Prairie Traveler* (1859), Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/23066/23066-h/23066-h.htm) · [Denig, *Indian Tribes of the Upper Missouri*, Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/49557/49557-h/49557-h.htm) · [Parkman, *The Oregon Trail* (1849), Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/1015/1015-h/1015-h.htm) · [Kurz, *Journal*, BAE Bull. 115, archive.org](https://archive.org/details/journalofrudolph00kurz) · [Boller, *Among the Indians* (1868), archive.org](https://archive.org/details/amongindians00boll) · [Fletcher, "White Buffalo Festival of the Uncpapas," Peabody 16th Ann. Rpt., archive.org](https://archive.org/details/annualreportoftr03peabuoft) · [Catlin, Letters and Notes, Letter 19](https://user.xmission.com/~drudy/mtman/html/catlin/letter19.html) · [Hornaday, *Extermination of the American Bison* (1889), Gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17748) · [Warren 1857 / Bear's Rib episode, HistoryNet](https://historynet.com/bear-butte-sacred-cheyennes-lakotas-looms-near-black-hills/) · [White buffalo prices and cases, All About Bison (citing Dary)](https://allaboutbison.com/white-buffalo/) · [Fort Union robe volume, NPS](https://www.nps.gov/articles/ftutrading.htm)</result>

## ASSISTANT

Research banked (e2c5c23). Here's the package — three insertions, every element attested, nothing ceremonial, no one ventriloquized. Each also closes one of the bonus holes:

**S1 — the stake renewed (Morrow-side, JE VIII Jan 1 entry, after "I need calm—so that my movements are not masked by wind or noise."):**

> The stake is still on the ridge above the valley, three winters old now. But it is not the same stake. Someone has drawn the old one and laid it in the grass, and set a new one, peeled white, the cloth at its head still bright. Beside it there are two buffalo skulls now, set side by side facing the valley, a band of scarlet bound around each horn. I did not touch any of it. I made my camp farther along.
>
> They have been keeping this valley longer than I have been keeping her.

Boller's exact sign (skulls, scarlet on the horns, facing the herd's ground), rendered as maintained practice — the old stake *drawn and replaced* makes three years of attention legible in one object. "I did not touch any of it" repeats his established mode verbatim from 08:65, and the closing line relativizes his whole vigil inside theirs without him understanding what he's said. This is the reviewer's repair in Morrow's own ledger-grammar. *(Closes: forbearance-as-decision; quietly, the herd's untouched winters.)*

**S2 — the flanking tracks (Farrell-side, Ch 9, in the trail section after "He was waiting for something."):**

> Twice that day I crossed the tracks of horses, small and unshod, four or five together, keeping to the high ground along the valley's grain. No pole-drags—men riding light. The tracks were days old, and they ran the way I was running.

Pure Marcy vocabulary (unshod and small, no lodge-pole drags = men not families, riding light), in Farrell's forensic register, no conclusion drawn — the reader assembles that the pursuit was never unobserved, and it retroactively seeds the riders' arrival two chapters later. *(Closes: the escort arriving "on cue" — they've been on the page since Chapter Nine.)*

**S3 — the price of her (rumor chain, the trapper at Fort Kearny, extending his speech after "It was the animal I couldn't look away from."):**

> "I'll tell you what else. A hide like that is worth a year of ordinary robes to the right buyer. There's men would go north for it. I gave it thought myself, for about as long as it took to remember whose country she's walking in."

The demand side attested (Maximilian's fifteen horses, Kurz's two racehorses), the temptation admitted — Kurz's Spaniard lives in "I gave it thought myself" — and the deterrent named as Lakota presence, matter-of-fact, no menace-trope. *(Closes: the fortune-on-the-hoof hole — the book's commercial world now sees her and explains why it doesn't come.)*

Notes on the package: S1 sits in JE VIII (not floored JE VI); all three are additive with the usual shift bookkeeping; the Kurz white-calfskin-stolen-from-a-hilltop is banked in the research note as a possible deep allusion but deliberately unused — one sign too pointed for now. Fletcher's "chief of the herd" turns out to already be in the book at 04:11, which I've noted — the herd-deference canon has a citation it never knew about.

Pick, edit, or strike any of the three.
